An upgrade that replaces the files at the same real path is seen, as before. **The installs, as lane 1 gives them** from the code at line R's merge166, which lane 5 has not read (#165 6895008151): `openRepoTools --install` places 17 commands, `lanes-edit.sh` and `lanes-index` among them, into `${OPENREPOTOOLS_BIN_DIR:-~/.local/bin}`, each by `place_by_rename`, a temporary file and then `mv -f`, so an upgrade replaces each file at the same real path, with a new inode, and is seen; it refuses to install where a command's path is a symbolic link; and the one link, `~/projects/xFactory/lanes-edit.sh`, which the hooks call, is repointed by `link-estates` only where it points somewhere else, printing "RELINK", in the move off the old checkout layout or after `OPENREPOTOOLS_BIN_DIR` changes. So no v1.3.0 upgrade step repoints a link to `lanes-index`. Nothing regressed: with a real path, `abspath` and `__file__` as given are the same path, so the limit is the same at `23cb3799` and before. **The remedy,** in lane 3's words: "stop the other install's service, whose `pid=` is in the owner file, or do not run two installs against one register at once" (#165 6895062857; section 2.4). Lane 3's rulings state the limit, with its consequence and its remedy, here, in `register_serve_launch`'s docstring and in the manual (#165 6894714082 and 6895062857). The docstring states it at T512's `4444ddc4` (`lanes-index`:7776–7813), as T512's commits 6, 8, 9 and 10 build it: the same four cases as here, and lane 3's remedy word for word; its consequence is in its own words, which agree with lane 3's; it states the working directory's link (:7792–7797); and `register_serve_launch_file`'s docstring (:8169–8188) states the `PATH_MAX` case; option 3, a client that finds another install reads directly, is a named follow-up after v1.3.0, in its own PR, and covers every case (#165 6895062857); and since no v1.3.0 upgrade step repoints a link to `lanes-index` (lane 1's answer, above), option 1 stands with no new ruling (#165 6895062857).
- **A configuration change.** Y has the service re-read `lanes-index.conf` and its password file when their mtime changes, and exit if the mode is no longer `primary` (Y:3918–3920). Lane 3's ruling on OP-18, (b), widens what it watches, and how: "each of the five changes what a new connection does", and "the ctime catches an edit that puts the mtime back" (#165 6880543448; the Codex re-review, B2). The next six sub-bullets are that rule, with lane 5's details.
  - **Which files it watches** (OP-18, closed on (b)). Up to five: always the configuration file; the reader password file, where `reader_passfile=` names one; and in addition each of `sslrootcert`, `sslcert` and `sslkey` that the configuration's `reader_url=` names. A TLS parameter the URL does not name, or names with an empty value, adds nothing, and nor does `sslrootcert=system`, that value exactly, which names the system's store and no file. Every other value of the three is watched literally, as a path, as the URL's mapping gives it to the child (`pg_url_env`'s `PGSSLROOTCERT`, `PGSSLCERT` and `PGSSLKEY`), whether or not libpq takes it as a file, an `sslkey=` in engine form included, below; the service classes no other value as "not a file path" (`register_serve_watched`, `lanes-index`:8167–8173 at T512's `bb19b1ad`; lane 3's Codex delta review of draft 16, SHOULD 3, #165 6885771975). Where `reader_passfile=` is not set, the child's `PGPASSFILE` is the service's own empty file (section 7), which it opens again, emptied, at each child's start, so its contents never change but its times do: there is no password file to watch, and watching that one would read each start as a change (lane 5's errata, item 2; `_no_password_file`, `lanes-index`:5130–5142 at T512's `26c77534`). The reader password file watched is the path `load_config` takes from `reader_passfile=`, a leading `~/` expanded to the home directory (`lanes-index`:710–711), which is also the path the child's `PGPASSFILE` gets (section 7). As built at T512's `26c77534` (`register_serve_watched`, `lanes-index`:8145–8172, its commits `b9c34674` and `aea52c9f`), with its comment on the `~/` at `f21ca4a7` (:8160–8166). An `sslkey=` may also name a key from an OpenSSL engine, "a colon-separated engine name and an engine-specific key identifier" (libpq's documentation, `sslkey`). libpq's test: where it is built with `USE_SSL_ENGINE`, it takes a value with a `:` in it as an engine's name and key, but on Windows not where the `:` is the second character, a drive's letter, and any other value as a file name, and it reads a key only where it has loaded a client certificate (`fe-secure-openssl.c`:1255; :1249–1262 and :1340–1345, at PostgreSQL's `REL_16_4`; lane 1's U5 NIT-o, #165 6885309342; lane 1's U5 NIT-r, #165 6885968900). The service does not tell that form from a path: it watches the value as a path, resolved as "How a path resolves" says, below, which is right wherever libpq takes the value as a file name. Where libpq takes the value as an engine's key, that path normally names no file, so its snapshot is normally `absent`, and stays so, and a change made inside the engine is not seen: the contract accepts that as a limit (lane 1's U5 NIT-k and NIT-l, #165 6883749855; lane 5's read-only review of round 3; unchanged at T512's `bb19b1ad`). Where a file does exist at that path, under `/`, the started service's directory, for a relative value, it is watched, harmlessly: libpq does not read it, and a change to it costs one rollover, as "Any other change retires the held child first" says, below, never a wrong answer (lane 1's U5 NIT-o).
  - **What it does not watch: what libpq finds on its own,** the default files under `~/.postgresql/`, the `PGSSL*` and other `PG*` variables, and a service file. This is a limit of the contract. A change to a default file under `~/.postgresql/` takes effect at the next child the service starts, as before, and until then the held child keeps what it read. The variables and a service file never reach the child: the service drops every inherited `PG` variable before it starts one, `PGSERVICE` and `PGSERVICEFILE` among them, and refuses a URL that names any parameter but the six (section 7).
  - **How a path resolves:** as libpq in the `psql` child resolves it. An absolute path is taken as written. A relative path resolves against the working directory the service starts its child in, which is the service's own, since the child is started with no directory of its own, and `lanes-index` never changes its own (`spawn`, `lanes-index`:8418–8428 at T512's `26c77534`, :8294–8297 at `88b50886`). That is `/` wherever the starter starts the service (section 8.2; `lanes-index`:7761–7764 at `26c77534`, :7718–7721 at `88b50886`), and the directory it was run from for a `serve` run by hand. The URL's TLS paths are taken as written, with no `~` and no expansion of a variable, as libpq takes them; `reader_passfile=`'s leading `~/` is expanded by `load_config` (`lanes-index`:710–711), so the password file watched is that expanded path, which the child's `PGPASSFILE` also gets, and which libpq in the child then resolves (`register_serve_watched`'s comment, :8160–8166 at T512's `f21ca4a7`). That is lane 5's settled rule 3, a path resolved as libpq in the `psql` child resolves it, stated whole, and no new rule (lane 5's reading). The direct read's `psql` resolves the same relative `sslrootcert`, `sslcert` or `sslkey` in its caller's directory, since `pg_url_env` passes each through as written (`lanes-index`:346–350), so a relative path can name one file to the service and another to the direct read (lane 1's U5 NIT-g, #165 6881937840; lane 5's answer, #165 6881986067). The failure is safe: a refused configuration or a failed connect, and then the direct read. The set of watched paths is computed from the configuration each time the configuration is read, so a rollover that changes the URL changes the set.
  - **A path's snapshot,** from one `os.stat`, which follows symbolic links as libpq's open does: `absent` where it fails with `ENOENT` or `ENOTDIR`; `error <errno name>` where it fails in any other way; and otherwise the identity `(st_dev, st_ino, st_size, st_mtime_ns, st_ctime_ns)`. A file replaced by `rename` has a new inode, and a file written in place a new ctime, which no user can set back, so a kept mtime, by `cp -p` or `rsync -t` for instance, hides no change. As built at T512's `26c77534` (`register_serve_snapshot`, `lanes-index`:8175–8188).
  - **What is a change:** any difference between the snapshot last taken and the current one, for any watched path: a missing file that appears, a file that goes missing, any change of error, and a change in which paths are watched among them. A change is a configuration change, as this section handles it, and starts B2's one step (below). It is not each file's sha256, OP-18's (c): the stat test catches the same edits, and hashing five files on every check is a cost the service need not pay (lane 3's ruling). As built at T512's `26c77534`: the stamp is each watched path with its snapshot, in order (`register_serve_config_stamp`, `lanes-index`:8191–8199).
  - **When it looks,** as before: on each request, before the request is queued. It takes each snapshot before the file is read, so a change made while the files are read counts at the next request. As built at T512's `26c77534`: each connection compares the stamp before it queues its request (`answer`, `lanes-index`:8991–9002), and `register_serve_load` takes the configuration's snapshot before it reads the configuration, and the other paths' from the configuration it read, before the child that reads them starts (:8202–8213). At `88b50886` the stamp was the two files' mtimes alone, in whole nanoseconds, the configuration's taken before it read the file (`register_serve_config_stamp` and `register_serve_load`, :8100–8121, compared at :8812–8826; :8078–8099 and :8746–8760 at line R's merge106), so a replaced TLS file, and an edit that kept the mtime, went unseen.
  - **A change that ends it.** A configuration that is now refused, or a mode other than `primary`, answers that request `unavailable restarting`, and the service exits. `unavailable restarting` is for that change, and for an install change, only (13.5): a stop of any other cause is plain `unavailable`. As built at T512's `bb19b1ad` (its commits `28777868` and `7ca56a40`): the request that saw the change is counted, asks the worker for it, and waits until the worker has taken it, until a `take_change` that began after it asked has finished, one that finds no change among them, or until the service stops; at most its timer, the kill's 100 ms and 1 s more, about 1.3 s at the 200 ms timer, or, where the worker is busy as the request asks, with a request's query, the idle check or a background fetch, its timer alone, from the request's arrival, which is 100 ms shorter than the client's wait but starts later, so that the client normally hears the answer, and otherwise gives up and reads directly (section 6.2; `answer`, `lanes-index`:9025–9075; `take_change`, :9077–9107). `take_change` records a change that ends the service, `change_ends`, under the same hold of `cond` that ends those waits, and only then stops the service; and the waiting request is answered `unavailable restarting` only where `change_ends` is set when its wait ends, so an idle stop, a signal or an ended worker during the wait is plain `unavailable` (:9073–9074 and :9104; lane 1's U5 NIT-A, #165 6883749855; lane 5's read-only review of round 3). As built at T512's `1e1846e3`, its round 5 (commit `30ee202f`): `take_change` records an ending change for its own requests only, the counts after the last take's and up to its own, `(taken, upto]`, and a waiting request answers `unavailable restarting` only where its own count falls in that range (`change_ends`, `lanes-index`:8273 and :9143–9146; `answer`, :9109–9110). At `bb19b1ad` one `change_ends` stood for the service, so a request whose own change did not end the service could hear `unavailable restarting` where a later change that did was taken before that request woke (Codex's review of T512 at `bb19b1ad`, NIT 5; :8272, :9070–9074 and :9101–9105 there). The contract accepts two limits, which no request can know sooner: (i) a request that sees a change while the worker is busy is answered within its own timer, and hears `unavailable restarting` only where the worker ends its job and takes the change within that timer, so where the job outlasts it, even a change that ends the service is plain `unavailable`; and (ii) a change that ends the service, but which the worker reaches after the wait's limit, is plain `unavailable`. Either way the service still stops once the worker takes the change. At `f21ca4a7` the word followed whether the service was stopping, for any cause, and a wait ran to its whole limit while the worker was busy, or where `take_change` found no change (:9014–9017 at `26c77534`, :9026–9029 at `f21ca4a7`).
  - **Any other change retires the held child first** (the Codex review, BLOCKING 6). The service takes the configuration as re-read, ends the held child at once, and starts a new one in the background with the new settings. It answers that request, and every request until the new child connects, `unavailable` (sections 5.3 and 7). So no answer is built on a child started before the change was seen (next bullet): a new `reader_url=`, `reader_passfile=`, password, schema, TLS parameter of the URL, or TLS file it names holds from that request on, as it does for the direct read (`lanes-index`:8762–8776, :8370–8375 and :8778–8790 at line R's merge106, :8828–8865 and :8404–8409 at T512's `88b50886`). One thread re-reads a changed configuration, the worker: a connection that sees the change asks the worker for it and waits, and the worker re-reads the configuration only where the stamp still differs, so requests that saw one change make one step between them, and no connection thread waits on a re-read or a kill (lane 1's gate, M1, N1 and N7, #165 6881993079; `answer`, `take_change` and `reload`, `lanes-index`:8991–9066 at T512's `26c77534`, its commit `85489345`).
  - **In one step, under one epoch** (the Codex re-review, B2; a requirement ahead of the code, as lane 3 confirmed it, #165 6880543448). The service takes the re-read configuration and ends the held child in one step, under the lock its worker takes each request by, so no request is sent to the old child under the new configuration; and in the same step it moves its configuration epoch. A request is queued under the epoch it was checked against, and its answer is given only where that epoch still holds when the answer is given: a request queued or running under an earlier epoch is answered `unavailable`, whatever the old child returns. So once a request has seen a change, no answer built on the old settings is given; the check is made where the answer is given to the request, and the send to the client comes after it (lane 1's U5 NIT-m, #165 6883749855; `serve_job`, `lanes-index`:8780 at T512's `bb19b1ad`). As built at T512's `26c77534` (its commits `11ce854d` and `85489345`): `reload` runs in the worker, between its jobs, and under `cond`, the lock the worker takes each request by, it publishes the configuration, retires the held child, to be replaced at once with no 1 s wait, answers the requests queued under the earlier epoch `unavailable`, and moves the epoch; it then kills the old child, on which no exchange is running (`lanes-index`:9032–9066). Each job carries its epoch (`_RegisterServeJob`, :8063–8070); `request` answers `unavailable` where its epoch no longer holds (:9078), and `serve_job` gave an answer only under its own epoch (:8765). That held the rule for a queued request, but not for a running one: since `reload` runs in the worker between its jobs, the epoch cannot move while a query runs, so `serve_job`'s check always passed, and a request already running on the old child was answered from it after another request had seen the change (lane 5's read-only review of round 3). T512's round 3b holds it, as built at `f21ca4a7` (its commit `3f1fc241`): a request that sees a change sets `reload_due` under `cond` (`lanes-index`:9024), which only the worker clears, as it takes the re-read (:8621), one job at a time; and `serve_job` gives the query's answer only where its epoch holds and no change is waiting (:8777; its comment, :8761–8769). So a query running when another request sees a change ends after that moment, and its answer is refused: the request is answered plain `unavailable`, from `serve_job`'s `finally` (:8779–8780), the word this bullet gives a request running under an earlier epoch. **The gate** (draft 19): a request has seen a change from the moment it starts its comparison of the stamps, and until that comparison ends no answer is given on the old settings. So the request raises its check under `cond` before it compares, and lets it go under `cond` after, in the same hold that sets `reload_due` where it found a change; and an answer waits while any check is open, within its request's timer, and is given only where no check is open, its epoch holds and no change waits, else the request is answered `unavailable`. `cond` is never held across the comparison's `stat` calls. As built at T512's `1e1846e3`, its round 5 (commit `0fa293f9`): `config_checking`, `lanes-index`:8270; raised at :9056–9058, before the comparison at :9062, and let go at :9064–9067 where nothing changed, or at :9097–9101 with `reload_due`; and `serve_job`'s wait and test, :8782–8796. At `bb19b1ad` the request compared the stamps outside `cond` and published `reload_due` only afterwards, so an answer given between the two was given on the old settings after that request had seen the change (Codex's review of T512 at `bb19b1ad`, SHOULD 1; `lanes-index`:8779–8781, :9034–9036 and :9062–9066 there). The one step is unchanged, and so is the one thread that re-reads. The cost is one more `unavailable` where `take_change` then finds no change left. At `88b50886` the configuration was published (:8839–8840) before `reconnect` ended the child (:8841; :8389–8409), with no epoch on a request (:8019; :8844–8865).
  - **Between a change and the next request,** the idle check and a background fetch may still run on the old child. Neither answers a request, and a fetched image is answered only where it matches the digest a later request's own transaction reads (section 5.1; `bring`, `lanes-index`:8652–8691 at line R's merge106, :8716–8755 at T512's `88b50886` and :8893–8932 at `26c77534`; lane 1's gate, N3, #165 6881993079).

### 8.5 Only on primary

- **`serve` refuses with exit 2 unless the configuration says `primary`** (Y:3948–3949; tasks:1402), with the stamp `config-refused` (Y:3928–3929). It runs only on primary: it asks T105's mode function and refuses otherwise, in the U5 gate's words (L1:459; lane 1's U5 re-check, N-e, #165 6752336451).
- **Under Q6(c)'s refusal,** `load_config` "refuses with 2 for any subcommand but `mode` and `register read`" (Y:4872), so the refusing build's `serve` exits 2, with no departure from Y (R36; tasks:1403).
- **So it is dormant on every real host** until T328 lifts that refusal. Its behaviour is tested on lane 2's flipped copy of `lanes-index`, L2-0d (tasks:1404; Y:3951–3956).
- **Clients ask the socket only where the mode answer is `primary`** (Y:3948–3950; L5:405).

### 8.6 The failure stamp, `register.failed`

- **What is stamped:** only the causes that make `serve` exit before it listens: `path-too-long`, `config-refused` (a refused configuration, a mode other than `primary`, or a URL parameter SVC-2 does not map) and `no-psql` (Y:3926–3929).
  - **`no-psql` covers a `psql` older than 15 too** (lane 3's ruling, #165 6754338646, OP-10; DEC-31). `serve` checks `psql --version` once, at start, before it listens, by T106's version check, with the same parser and the same floor of 15, which `serve` reuses (CLI411:234), so the two ends cannot disagree on a vendor's version string, such as Homebrew's (lane 1's U5 review of draft 8, N-5, #165 6828035829). Where the version is older than 15, it stamps `no-psql` and exits 2, as it does with no `psql`. So `no-psql` reads "no usable `psql`".
  - **The 2** is `exit-codes.md`'s `no-psql` row (B19-exit-codes:226), which lane 1's T102 follow-up gives, on lane 5's proposal (#165 6746948770; #165 6746631207). Y:3926–3931 says only "then exits" (lane 1's U5 review of draft 8, N-4, #165 6828035829).
  - **Why:** the request's statements are joined with psql's `\;` and sent as one query (section 5.1; Y:3985–3988), and that join needs `psql` 15 or later (CLI411:234). A `psql` that cannot join a request's statements cannot serve.
  - **Y's fixed cause list stays as it is** (Y:3926–3931). No cause word is added.
  - **Lane 2's check:** lane 2's T237 checks this with one case, `[psql-14]` of `test_a_service_that_cannot_start_falls_back_to_the_direct_read`, in `test_sql_register_guard.py`, which fails until this check lands with L5-S1 (lane 2, #165 6754384610).
- **Never `dir-check`.** Y:3928 also names `dir-check`, but `register.failed` is a file in that same directory (Y:3767–3768), which is never used once it fails its check (Y:3764–3765). Lane 3's ruling takes it out, as an erratum on Y:3927–3929, ERR-28: `serve` exits 2 naming the directory and writes nothing in it, and `lanes-index status` reports the failure itself (#165 6746215510, OP-9; section 2.2).
- **What it holds:** the UTC and that fixed cause, and no other text (Y:3929–3931).
  - **Its bytes, as T512 writes them: the writer's grammar** (the Codex review, BLOCKING 4; `lanes-index`:7592–7600 at line R's merge106): one ASCII line, `<UTC> <cause>\n`, the UTC as `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ` in whole seconds, one space, and the cause, one of the three words above. It carries no version: both ends are one install, and a stamp outside the reader's grammar is no stamp (below).
  - **How it is written** (`lanes-index`:7575–7600): whole, mode 0600, by a temporary file beside it and `rename`, so a reader finds the old stamp or the new, never part of one. A stamp that cannot be written is passed over, and `serve` still exits 2. It is written only while `serve` holds `register.lock`, which it takes without waiting: where another process holds the lock, a live service among them, no stamp is written, so a start made beside a healthy service never leaves a stamp that would hold starts back (lane 1's N9, #165 6882025131; `register_serve_stamp`, `lanes-index`:7592–7622 at T512's `26c77534`, its commit `bb898de4`). Where `register.lock` cannot be opened, for `EACCES` or `ELOOP` for instance, or the platform has no `flock`, the stamp is written without the lock, as it was before round 3, even beside a live service (`lanes-index`:7605–7617 at T512's `bb19b1ad`, the same lines since `26c77534`). The code's comment gives no reason but "as before" (:7592–7604); lane 5's is that the stamp still spares starts that would fail for its cause, while a live service is not affected, since a client reads the stamp only after its connect fails ("Who reads it", below). The contract accepts that as a limit: `lanes-index status` may then show a stamp beside a live service (lane 1's U5 NIT-k, #165 6883749855; lane 5's read-only review of round 3).
  - **What a reader takes: the reader's grammar** (the Codex re-review, B3; as built, `lanes-index`:1817–1833, :7603–7624 and :8898–8904 at line R's merge106, unchanged at T512's `88b50886`). It reads at most the stamp's first 200 bytes, as ASCII, and splits them on whitespace. A stamp is exactly two words: a UTC, as `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ` or `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MMZ`, that names a real time; and a cause, any word, one of the three or not. Anything else is no stamp: a file that is absent, cannot be read, is not a regular file or is not ASCII, a count of words other than two, or a UTC that does not parse. Since T512's `26c77534` it is read only as a regular file, as the owner file is (section 2.4; lane 1's N6, #165 6881993079; `register_serve_stamp_read`, `lanes-index`:7646–7660). The UTC is parsed by Python's `strptime` in one of the two forms (`register_utc_epoch`, :1817–1833), which also takes unpadded fields, such as `2026-1-5T1:2:3Z`, so the reader is wider than the two forms; that is safe, as the next bullet says of every stamp in the reader's grammar, and no code changes for it (lane 1's U5 NIT-i, #165 6881937840; lane 5's answer, #165 6881986067).
  - **Why the reader is wider than the writer.** Both outcomes are safe, since the stamp only spares starts. A stamp in the reader's grammar, with a minute's precision or a cause the writer never writes, still holds starts back for 60 s, which costs direct reads; a stamp outside it is no stamp, so a start goes ahead, which costs one Python start. Neither gives a wrong answer. A stamp dated ahead of the reader's clock is a stamp, but counts as old, so a clock step never holds starts back past the 60 s. `lanes-index status` shows a stamp's cause, whatever the word.
- **What is not stamped:** a database that refuses or does not answer (`auth`, `unreachable`, a refused schema version). The service listens, answers `refuse db <cause>` or `unavailable`, and reconnects in the background (Y:3931–3934).
- **Who reads it:** a client, only after `ENOENT` or `ECONNREFUSED`; a stamp younger than 60 s means it starts nothing and reads directly (Y:3934–3936; tasks:1400). And `lanes-index status`, which reports a stamp and its cause (Y:3939), and a directory that fails its check, which has no stamp (OP-9), in T512's lines in `cmd_status` (R44; tasks:1419).
- **When it goes:** the service removes any stamp once it listens, and again at its first successful connect (Y:3936–3938).
- **No cause is open:** `no-psql` (OP-10) is closed by lane 3's ruling, #165 6754633102, on lane 2's view, #165 6754620468. With no `psql` at start, or no usable one, `serve` stamps `no-psql` and exits 2 before it listens (B19-exit-codes:226), so it never answers, `refuse` included (Y:3926–3931). The test plan's "answers `refuse`" (tp:2028–2030) gives way to it, by lane 3's erratum, ERR-45, which round B9 records. OP-10's older-`psql` half was ruled (a) in draft 7 (lane 3's ruling, #165 6754338646; above). What the operator sees then: the client reads directly, as after any stamp, and that direct read's own check refuses with 69, naming the version, with nothing sent (lane 3's ruling, #165 6752697506; CLI411:234; ERR-44), so the version is still shown. That is lane 3's assembly reading, DEC-31, "a decision, not an erratum", and round B9 records it in `research.md`, beside ERR-44's erratum. Lane 2's T237 test `test_the_service_reaches_the_database_only_through_psql` keeps its first half, that every answer from the database is a call in the fake's log, and its no-`psql` half asserts the stamp and the exit before any socket exists, then the client's direct read answering (6754633102). 007 adds no case for a `psql` lost after the start (6754633102), which is SVC-6's failure, with no stamp (section 7). `dir-check` is closed (OP-9).

## 9. The start-only form: `lanes-index serve --start` (R50)

- **What it does:** it runs the starter of section 8.2, which starts the launcher by its path as invoked, made absolute with no lexical normalization, and returns at once, so the bash `SessionStart` block detaches nothing itself (tasks:1401; R50, M:714–720; Y:3892–3893).
- **What it does not do:** ask the service anything, or wait for it (svc:93; Y:4493–4499; tasks:1436).
- **What it runs** is "the start a client runs on `ENOENT`" (Y:4498–4499): the length and directory checks of section 2, the stamp of section 8.6, then the start. It makes no socket try of its own, and runs the client's start path (Y:4497–4499). A live service is left as it is: the `serve` it starts finds `register.lock` held, and exits 0 (section 8.3). This bullet and the next two are lane 5's decision, which adopts lane 1's review position as the contract's text (lane 1's answer, #165 6746620707, OP-13).
- **Its exits:**
  - **0** where it launched `serve`, or where a failure stamp younger than 60 s stands, so that it starts none (Y:3934–3936; section 8.6). Those are its two exit-0 cases, as lane 1 confirmed them (#165 6747475637);
  - **2** for a refused configuration, Q6(c)'s included (Y:4872), a mode other than `primary` (Y:3948–3949), or a failed path or directory check (section 2); a failed directory check is never stamped, by lane 3's OP-9 ruling (section 2.2); and where `serve` could not be launched, its `register.log` not opened or its start failing, or where the platform has no Unix socket (lane 1's N9, #165 6882025131; `lanes-index`:7783–7785 and :7808–7811 at T512's `26c77534`, as at `88b50886`);
  - **64** for usage.
- **No stamp of its own.** `serve --start` writes no `register.failed`; the `serve` it launches stamps as section 8.6 says.
- **It never meets OP-8's start-none case** (OP-16, closed). That case applies only to a socket that answers: `ENOENT` or `ECONNREFUSED` starts one whatever the owner file says, and only a socket that answers with an owner file from another namespace or boot starts none (lane 3's correction, #165 6746655077; section 2.4). `serve --start` makes no socket try, and runs the start a client runs on `ENOENT` (Y:4497–4499), so it launches `serve`, which exits 0 where another namespace's service holds a shared `register.lock` (section 8.3), and binds where the owner file is stale. So a stale owner file from before a reboot never stops the warm start. Lane 1 confirmed this reading (#165 6747475637), and withdrew one clause of its answer's exit-0 line, "or lane 3's OP-8 case" (#165 6746620707, its :25), since it rested on the OP-8 text lane 3 corrected. Lane 3's corrected OP-8 agrees (#165 6747456383). Lane 1's U5 re-check finds that the clause was quoted two ways for one case, and that keeping the posted words is the more exact choice (#165 6752336451).
- **Its caller** is T513's block, on a `mode=primary` workstation, after its own answer, without waiting (svc:93; tasks:1432). The block exits 0 always (svc:93), so it never branches on `serve --start`'s exit; R36 reads the block the same way (M:594).
- **Under Q6(c)'s refusal** it exits 2 at load, as every subcommand but `mode` and `register read` does (Y:4872). Before group C no real host is `primary`, so the block's start never fires there (tasks:1437).

## 10. The two test-only variables (R37)

- **`LANES_REGISTER_SERVE_IDLE_CHECK_S`:** the interval of section 7's `SELECT 1`, default 60 (tasks:1421; Y:3994).
- **`LANES_REGISTER_SERVE_IDLE_STOP_S`:** section 8.4's idle stop, default 900 (tasks:1422; Y:3912).
- **Test-only** (R37; research.md DEC-15). They shorten AT-10's idle-check case and the idle-stop guess (tasks:1428).
- **Honoured only where set.** Each names whole seconds. A value that is not a positive whole number is ignored, and the default kept, so each has a floor of 1 s (tasks:1423; R37, M:603). A value under the floor is ignored and the default kept, never `config-refused` (lane 3's ruling, #165 6746215510, OP-14; Y:3928–3929). DEC-15 gains "a value that is not a positive whole number is ignored, and the default kept", in B4.
- **The idle check's cap:** below 240 s, the load balancer's idle reset. A value of 240 or more is ignored, and 60 kept (tasks:1423; R37; DEC-15; Y:3995–3997). The idle stop has no cap.
- **The starter keeps both** in its reduced environment where they are set, or the service never sees them (tasks:1427; R37; DEC-15).
- **R37 says "refuse" where T512 says "ignored":** R37's "refuse a value under 1 s" means the value is ignored and the default kept (lane 3's ruling, #165 6746215510, OP-14). OP-14 is closed.

## 11. The client's side, as far as the socket fixes it

### 11.1 One client

- **`lanes-index register read` asks the socket first,** and reads directly when the service is unavailable (Y:3843–3845; tasks:1394). "One socket client, one fallback, one set of timeouts" (Y:3847–3848).
- **Phase 1's `register read` has only its direct path.** The socket, the `.direct` flag and the write-through come with `serve` (Y:4868; CLI46:653). Phase 1 fixes what `serve` must also return: "the files at `n` with per-file digests, written by the client into the cache files" (Y:4868).
- **By form** (CLI44:655–662): form 1, the readers' prefetch, asks the socket; form 2, the guard's prefetch, asks it; form 3, the readers' second read, asks it first, and reads `.direct` as forms 1 and 2 do (lane 1's answer, #165 6746620707, OP-7; CLI47:730); form 4 never asks it (B7). Section 13.9 says what each form sends.

### 11.2 The fallback, and its line

- **The fallback is the direct read** (svc:104; Y:3843–3845). On `mode=primary` it reads the database, never the mirror, except under a declared outage, whose three conditions the client checks, never the service (svc:104; Y:4441–4443).
- **The guard's fallback line,** on stderr, exactly:

  ```text
  guard: direct (service unavailable)
  ```

  `--for guard` sets it (Y:3845–3848). Form 2 prints it once, wherever the service was unavailable and the client then read directly, whether that read succeeded (Y:4342) or failed (svc:104, svc:116–121; tp:1846–1849). An owner file the client cannot use, or one of another namespace or boot, is such a case, and so is a connection the service closes past its cap (sections 2.4 and 7). It never comes after a `refuse db <cause>`, which is not unavailable and is followed by no direct read (Y:4085–4086). On a 69 it follows the cause line, which stays first (section 5.2). That is lane 1's answer (#165 6746620707, OP-11; CLI46:764, CLI47:767).
- **Test 10's comparison.** Through the service and through the direct read, the guard's stdout, its stderr less this one line, and its exit code are equal, for `ok`, `refuse` and `offer` (svc:86–90; tp:1912–1916).

### 11.3 The service-down flag, `.direct`

- **A file,** `$SE_CACHE_FILE.direct`, written by the first fallback in the invocation. Every later read in that invocation, inside any subshell, finds it and goes straight to the direct path (Y:4153–4156; tasks:1413). Forms 1, 2 and 3 read it (CLI44:688; CLI47:730). An owner file the client cannot use, one of another namespace or boot, and a connection closed past the service's cap are fallbacks like any other, so each writes it (sections 2.4 and 7; `lanes-index`:9436–9481 at T512's `88b50886`).
- **Form 4 never reads it** (B7; CLI44:688). `reg_flush` keeps it (CLI44:815).
- **A request the client does not send writes none.** Where a request, its `end` line included, would pass 65,536 bytes, the client reads directly by its own decision, and writes no `.direct`, since the service is not at fault (lane 1's U5 review, N5; section 13.3). A form-3 call with a `--log-key=` key the request cannot carry is the same, which lane 1 takes (lane 1's U5 re-check, #165 6752336451; CLI411:688; section 13.9).

### 11.4 `w_s`, the client's wait, in `.head`

- **`.head`'s `w_s_ms=`,** in whole milliseconds: 300, or `register_rtt_ms=` plus 200 where `register_rtt_ms=` is over 100 (CLI44:298–301; Y:5457).
- **Wherever this `lanes-index` has `serve`, on primary,** whether or not the service answered this read (O16; tasks:1415; CLI44:303). It is 0 where no service can answer (CLI44:302).
- **The guard copies it** into the bind record, and `guard-bind` sums it into `bound_s` (CLI44:304; Y:5459).

### 11.5 Host-local facts stay with the client

- **The guard keeps them:** the hook's `session_id`, `cwd`, `agent_id` and `prompt`, the window reference and name, and the live record's `name`, `nameSource` and liveness facts, read from this container's session records and tmux (svc:59; Y:4430–4435). It sends none of them (section 4).
- **The guard decides.** It runs today's parser on the text and exits 0 or 2; the offer file, the `/rename` typing and answer 2's writes stay client-side, run detached from the hook (svc:59; Y:4166–4173).
- **The declared outage is the client's.** The client reads the declaration, never the service (Y:4441–4443, Y:4476–4478).
- **`CLAUDE_NO_LANE=1`** exits 0 before any input is read, with no service contacted and nothing started (svc:79–83; Y:4290–4292).
